Start with Richard Cantillon! The true father of economics. He invented the law of supply and demand and the eponymous Cantillon Effect. 

Adam Smith comes later and tries to apply Newtonian physics to economics, a sort of spell we are still under..

Cantillon axiomatizes economics in the spirit of Euclid. He identifies entrepreneurs as agents of price discovery across markets.

> ​@PredictiveHistory For what it's worth, the popular Austrian School of economics traces its lineage to Richard Cantillon and the original French School of economics.   
>   
> The French School were like the Presocratics, applying deductive reasoning to empirical phenomena, whereas the British School from Adam Smith onwards were like Plato, imposing a top-down structure. The 'Invisible Hand' was like the 'Theory of the Forms', the basis of the doctrine (or ideology even) because it made the system work, begging the question as it were.   
>   
> From Newton onward, every academic discipline tried to justify itself as a science in the sense of Newtonian physics, with axiomatic principles laid at from the outset and imposed on empirical phenomena. (This works well with physics when you have reproducible data, but less so in economics when the data is based on human choice).  The method of gradually inducting general principles became less popular, and the British school won the day like Wellington at Waterloo.  
>

Maybe perhaps building a new curriculum called predictive history, not only can we prepare and adjust to future history but maybe, just may be able to change the collective history of mankind.  What we are witnessing in middle east is very tragic. It is avoidable, in my opinion.   Also why must China and USA have to compete with each other in a zero sum game assumptions. But what if 60-80% of all humanity got to hear from professor Jiang and understood the game we are all part of and don't let the elites control our collective destiny. ### **@dimitristsakatsonis5116** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=voQEteh6Hko&lc=UgzkgVvYCstwFZ7hTpN4AaABAg)

In a previous comment I criticized the professor for not being scientific or for not using scientific methods. That criticism comes from the way I was taught to recognize truth and valid knowledge. For most of my life, I have learned to trust knowledge that comes from experts who can present evidence, conduct deep and systematic analysis, welcome criticism, and usually work on topics that are not considered highly controversial.

But this raises an interesting question: how do we actually learn, and who do we trust in order to learn? Our first unquestioned source of knowledge is our parents. The model they provide shapes, to some degree, the kinds of people we later allow ourselves to learn from. As we mature, we gain enough experience to reassess and reconstruct our belief system. Yet that belief system is always a function of two forces: ourselves (our internal reasoning) and the society or world around us (external influence).

My point is that we have learned to value knowledge coming from a very specific type of person—credentialed, institutional, academically recognized. But reality is more complex. If we encountered someone claiming to be a “wizard” with knowledge of the metaphysical world, we would probably reject it immediately. Still, there is often a brief moment where we pause and question before returning to our familiar frameworks of interpretation.

Novelty, however, has always been part of both personal and collective evolution. I am not saying that everything that can be said is true. But I do think it is reasonable to acknowledge that deception and manipulation are abundant in modern societies. That leaves us with a difficult question—perhaps the central intellectual challenge of our time: how do we define truth?

In that sense, the professor’s method of analysis may represent one possible path toward some kind of valid knowledge, even if it challenges conventional frameworks.

Consider this example: before today, if someone told me that there was a hidden elite manipulating society and engaging in extreme abuses and cannibalism, I would have dismissed it immediately as conspiracy theory. But events such as the revelations surrounding the Epstein case forced many people to reconsider the assumption that such things are impossible. These elites who practice certain rituals clearly do exist.   *parenthesis*[Corruption does not seem like a word strong enough to capture the essence of what is happening. As power grows, responsibility grows with it but limited human cognition cannot appreciate this amount of responsibility. It is like you, trying to drink an ocean instead of glass of water because you are thirsty. Those who possess immense wealth and authority—the people at the top—do not merely influence events; they shape the conditions under which entire societies live or die. In this sense, they are responsible not only for particular decisions but (as the professor might have said) for the very form of our modern civilization.
This point cannot be emphasized enough. The invocation of emotions is something that language has lost its ability to do. Yet try to imagine it in a different way: picture a cosmic ear suspended in space, listening to the Earth. What would it hear? A vast chorus of pain, fear, and struggle—an entire planet filled with cries of suffering. This is the “soul of the world".

Someone might reply, “But I feel fine. My life is comfortable. I have everything I need.” And the answer would be: yes, brother/ sister, perhaps you do. But put this into statistics. Then sincerely look in the mirror and ask yourself: are you merely a single carbon atom in your body—or are you the whole organism? Are you only matter, or are you also something else ?]*end_of_parenthesis*

So my suggestion to everyone here is simple: listen to Professor Jiang carefully and critically. Maintain skepticism, but also allow yourselves—at least temporarily—to step outside the intellectual habits and assumptions we inherited from institutions and academic traditions. Examine the ideas on their own terms and consider the possibility that some of what the professor proposes might contain elements of truth.

> @anonyshinki So far, the only English-language YouTube channel on Chinese history that I think is reasonably decent for beginners is “Cool History Bros”. It can at least scratch the surface.    
>   
> But honestly, I haven’t really seen many non-Chinese sources that explain Chinese history well. Chinese history is a civilization where language, philosophy, and institutions are deeply interconnected.  
>   
> If someone doesn’t understand classical Chinese literature, Chinese philosophical traditions, and the broader cultural context, and instead tries to interpret Chinese history purely through modern Western political science or Western theoretical frameworks, they often end up misreading it.  
>   
> Chinese history is also deeply tied to the tradition of classical Confucian scholarship, Daoism, Buddhism. Unlike the history of many countries that can be told mainly as a sequence of political events, Chinese history is strongly intertwined with intellectual and philosophical systems.  
>   
> In addition, many primary historical sources in China are written in extremely concise classical Chinese. This language is actually central to understanding Chinese history. A single short sentence in classical Chinese can contain a great deal of historical and cultural context. Without a real feel for classical Chinese, it is very easy to misunderstand the meaning.  

> **@crimsonitacilunarnebula**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=voQEteh6Hko&lc=UgxAsTj-OYG_GZXCTIt4AaABAg.AUDOBuBZbSqAUE_W7MfFaG)  
>
> Excellent question! Here is a breakdown of which dynasties were responsible for standardizing key aspects of Chinese civilization:  
>   
> ⚙️ The Great Standardizers  
>   
> Qin Dynasty (221–206 BCE)  
>   
> · The Unifiers: This dynasty was the first and most aggressive standardizer. After conquering the warring states, they found a region with different scripts, currencies, and even axle widths.  
>   · Writing: They unified the script into a standardized set of characters.  
>   · Currency: They created a standard round coin with a square hole.  
>   · Measurements: They unified weights and measures for trade and taxes.  
>   · Axle Lengths: They standardized axle lengths so carts could run in the same ruts across the empire.  
>   
> Han Dynasty (206 BCE–220 CE)  
>   
> · The Ideological Standardizers: The Han didn't standardize tools, but they standardized thought. They made Confucianism the official state ideology, creating a common set of values for government and society for the next 2,000 years.  
>   
> Sui Dynasty (581–618 CE)  
>   
> · The Infrastructure Standardizers: They didn't create new standards, but they connected the existing ones. By building the Grand Canal, they linked the standardized northern economies with the southern ones, creating a single economic zone.  
>   
> Tang Dynasty (618–907 CE)  
>   
> · The Meritocracy Standardizers: They perfected the civil service examination system. This standardized how officials were hired, ensuring they were all educated in the same Confucian classics.  
>   
> Song Dynasty (960–1279 CE)  
>   
> · The Financial Standardizers: Facing a copper shortage, they stepped in to standardize the economy by issuing the world's first government-backed paper money.  
>   
> Ming Dynasty (1368–1644 CE)  
>   
> · The Architectural Standardizers: They standardized the look of the Great Wall, rebuilding it in the brick-and-stone form we recognize today.  
>   
> Qing Dynasty (1644–1911 CE)  
>   
> · The Legal Standardizers: They maintained Ming laws but standardized the legal system across their vast, newly conquered territories (like Tibet and Xinjiang) to ensure consistent imperial control.  
>   
> Would you like to dive deeper into the specifics of Qin Shi Huang's unification policies?China's long history is a fascinating story of dynasties rising and falling, each learning from the mistakes of its predecessor to build a stronger, more stable civilization. Here is a summary of major dynasties, their contributions, and how they solved the problems of the previous age to create a better foundation for the future.  
>   
> 🏛️ The Early Foundational Dynasties  
>   
> · Xia Dynasty (c. 2070–1600 BCE): As China's first dynasty, its existence blends legend and history. It is credited with taming floods (which brought social stability) and establishing a hereditary system, setting the first template for dynastic rule .  
> · Shang Dynasty (c. 1600–1046 BCE): The Shang built upon the Xia framework. They developed a complex writing system on oracle bones, advanced bronze metallurgy for weapons and rituals, and created a stratified social structure, moving China from legend into verifiable history .  
> · Zhou Dynasty (c. 1046–256 BCE): The Zhou justified their rebellion against the Shang with the concept of the "Mandate of Heaven" —the idea that a ruler must be virtuous to rule. This became the moral bedrock for all future dynasties. They also introduced a feudal system, but over time, regional lords grew too powerful, leading to the chaotic Warring States period .  
>   
> ⚔️ The First Empire and Its Successor  
>   
> · Qin Dynasty (221–206 BCE): The Qin solved the problem of constant warfare by brutally conquering all rival states to unify China for the first time. To hold this new empire together, Qin Shi Huang, the First Emperor, standardized writing, currency, weights and measures, and even axle lengths . He also started linking existing walls to form the first Great Wall. However, the Qin's ultra-legalist, harsh, and oppressive rule created a new problem: widespread resentment .  
> · Han Dynasty (206 BCE–220 CE): The Han directly addressed the Qin's oppressive legacy. They kept the Qin's brilliant centralized administrative structure but softened its harshness by adopting Confucianism as the state ideology, which emphasized morality, education, and benevolent governance . This blend created a long-lasting golden age. They also presided over a boom in science and technology, inventing paper, the seismograph, and advanced ironworking .  
>   
> 🌸 The Age of Division and Cosmopolitan Reunification  
>   
> After the Han fell, China fragmented for about 400 years. This period saw the rise of temporary dynasties that grappled with integrating foreign cultures and re-establishing unified rule.  
>   
> · Sui Dynasty (581–618 CE): The short-lived Sui dynasty solved the problem of three centuries of disunity by reunifying China. They consolidated the state and undertook massive, crucial infrastructure projects, most notably the Grand Canal, to link the rich south with the political north . However, like the Qin, their projects and military campaigns were so brutal and demanding that they exhausted the people and the state's resources, leading to their rapid collapse .  
> · Tang Dynasty (618–907 CE): The Tang learned from the Sui's overreach. They maintained unification but governed with more moderation and efficiency. They perfected a civil service exam system based on Confucian texts, allowing talented commoners to become officials and reducing the power of old aristocratic families . This created a stable, professional government. The Tang capital, Chang'an, became a cosmopolitan hub on the Silk Road, and this era is celebrated as a golden age of poetry, art, and culture .  
>   
> 🎨 The Technological and Commercial Eras  
>   
> · Song Dynasty (960–1279 CE): The Tang eventually weakened due to military rebellions and internal strife. The Song solved the problem of regional military rule by putting civilian administrators firmly in charge of the military. This led to an unprecedented era of technological innovation (like movable type printing and gunpowder as a weapon), commercial expansion, and the world's first paper money . However, their focus on civil governance over military power left them vulnerable to northern invaders, who eventually conquered northern China .  
> · Yuan Dynasty (1271–1368 CE): The Mongol-led Yuan dynasty solved the problem of internal division by conquering all of China and creating a huge, unified empire. They improved overland trade routes, connecting China more closely to Eurasia . Yet, they maintained a strict racial hierarchy, with Mongols at the top and Chinese at the bottom, which created deep social resentment and instability .  
> · Ming Dynasty (1368–1644 CE): The Ming reasserted native Han Chinese rule, rejecting the Yuan's racial hierarchy. They solved the problem of foreign domination by rebuilding and reinforcing China's identity, completing the Great Wall as we know it today . They also launched massive voyages of exploration under Zheng He, showcasing China's power . However, they became increasingly inward-looking and isolationist later on.  
> · Qing Dynasty (1644–1911 CE): The Manchu-led Qing dynasty solved the problem of the Ming's collapse by once again reunifying China under a powerful, centralized state. They skillfully expanded China to its largest-ever territorial extent and adopted Confucian governance to legitimize their rule . For a time, this created a long period of stability and prosperity, but their resistance to change in the 19th century ultimately led to new challenges with the modern world.  
>   
> I hope this summary helps you see the bigger picture of Chinese history. Are you interested in a deeper look at any of these dynasties in particular?
